# THE WHIP
## OF DEMONS,
### CONTAINING TERRIBLE,
->most powerful, and effective exorcisms.<-
-><-

### BY THE REVEREND FATHER FRIAR JEROME
->*Menghi of Viadana, of the Order of Friars Minor*<-
->*of the Regular Observance.*<-
<note>Girolamo Menghi (1529–1609) was perhaps the most influential exorcist of the 16th century; his works were standard manuals for the Catholic Church for centuries.</note>

### TO THE MOST ILLUSTRIOUS,
### AND MOST REVEREND LORD,
### LORD GABRIEL PALEOTTI: OF THE TITLE
->*of Saint Lawrence in Lucina, Priest of the Holy Roman Church,*<-
->*most distinguished Cardinal, Archbishop*<-
->*of Bologna, and the*<-
->*Greatest Prince,*<-
<note>Gabriel Paleotti (1522–1597) was a central figure in the Counter-Reformation and a highly influential Archbishop of Bologna known for his work on sacred art and ecclesiastical reform.</note>

->Friar **JEROME MENGHI** of Viadana,<-
->of the Order of Friars Minor of the Regular Observance, sends greeting.<-

<image-desc>Ornamental woodcut drop cap 'I' decorated with intricate floral and leaf patterns.</image-desc>

*It is clearly established (Most Reverend Prelate) that, according to the opinion of the wisest men and as can be proven by many reasons, very many gifts of <term>God the Best and Greatest</term><note>original: "Dei Opt. Max." – a common Latin formula (Deo Optimo Maximo) often used to address the Christian God, borrowing a title from the Roman god Jupiter.</note> have been bestowed upon humans, by which each person might be able to benefit not only themselves, but also others to a very great degree. For this reason, it has come to pass that those who were concerned only for their own <term>salvation</term><note>In this context, salvation refers to both the spiritual safety of the soul and the physical health/deliverance of the person.</note>, and not that of others, have always been criticized by all,*

<summary>This page is the title and dedication leaf of Girolamo Menghi's "Flagellum Daemonum," a famous 16th-century manual on exorcism. The author dedicates the work to Cardinal Gabriel Paleotti, framing the practice of exorcism as a divine gift intended for the benefit of the wider community.</summary>
<keywords>exorcism, Girolamo Menghi, Gabriel Paleotti, Counter-Reformation, demonology, Bologna, Friars Minor, spiritual healing</keywords>

--- Page 2 ---
<meta>This page continues the dedication to Cardinal Gabriel Paleotti. The previous page ended with a discussion on those who seek only their own salvation being unworthy of human society. This page picks up with the contrast—praising those who dedicate their lives to helping others and explaining the author's motivation for writing on exorcism.</meta>

# PREFACE.

and were held worthy to be justly driven out from human society. On the other hand, that learned antiquity judged those who studiously labored to deserve well of others to be worthy of all praise; indeed, it even placed them among the number of the Gods. And truly, who would not freely praise and admire that race of men who directed all the powers of their intellect toward this end: that they might provide not only for the people of the present with their teaching, but also for posterity through accurately written books?

In whose number, without any controversy, those supreme <term>Theologians</term><note>Scholars who study the nature of God and religious belief.</note> must be placed first, who have written so many and such great works with no less learning than diligence, so that they might stir men both to worship God piously and to love their neighbor uniquely. Since I also did not wish to depart from their noble practice—even though I was not unaware of how small the powers of my intellect are, which demand rather that I be taught than that I teach—I have nevertheless decided, relying on the divine power, to undertake something in which all may easily understand and approve, if not my ability, then certainly my will in helping others.

And although many things came to mind as I pondered this very thing often and for a long time, I am nevertheless greatly moved because I see that we have fallen into these evil times, in which there wanders everywhere and most strongly flourishes a certain <term>sickness</term><note>original: "aegritudo" — here referring to a spiritual or supernatural affliction rather than a purely biological one.</note> which threatens danger and death not only to bodies, but also for the most part to souls. It is more destructive than every other kind of disease and must be dealt with. This is that [affliction] by which very many in various places are now being harassed, both long and bitterly, by <term>demons</term><note>original: "daemonib." — malevolent spiritual entities.</note> and by <term>sorcerers</term><note>original: "Maleficis" — individuals believed to perform harmful magic or witchcraft.</note>. Their misfortunes are the more to be pitied as, in these times of ours, there are fewer who [bring] some effective protection to them—

<summary>In this preface, Menghi argues that the highest calling is to help others through writing and theology. He explains his decision to write this manual as a response to a growing "sickness" of demonic possession and witchcraft that he believes is plaguing his time.</summary>
<keywords>theology, Girolamo Menghi, demons, sorcerers, spiritual sickness, exorcism, public good, posterity</keywords>

--- Page 3 ---
<meta>This page continues the preface. The author previously discussed his motivation to help others through writing, acknowledging his own modest intellect but relying on divine power to address the "sickness" of demonic affliction.</meta>

-># PREFACE.<-

...know how to bring help, or are willing to. For there are not lacking those who, if they have discovered anything by the kindness of God which is best for repelling this evil, conceal it; and, so as not to share it with others, they hide it in their own bosom. This they do led solely (not to say anything more severe) by a desire for <term>popular acclaim</term><note>original: "aurae popularis" — literally the "breeze of the people," referring to the fleeting and superficial nature of public favor.</note> and empty glory. Nor would there be lacking those who, moved by mercy, would freely undertake this so pious and necessary work, were they not deterred either by a certain fear (which nevertheless does not befit a Priest) or by an ignorance of the **exorcistic art**; especially since books pertaining to that art have, in our times, almost perished—both through the passage of time and, indeed, through the negligence of men. 

What of the fact that others, while they profess this art, so that they alone may seem to be wise and worthy of greater admiration, set aside the canons of this art established by our ancestors with right reason? They rashly flee to certain foolishnesses of their own and almost <term>old-womanish superstitions</term><note>original: "superstitiones penè aniles" — a common Renaissance-era pejorative for folk magic or unlearned beliefs deemed beneath the dignity of the Church.</note>, forgetting that a business of this kind can never be rightly accomplished by any other means than in the name of God. From this fact it happens that they eventually fall of their own accord into the extreme peril of their salvation; for they do not follow the ways of God as they ought, but follow the ways of demons (which, being blind, they do not notice), pursuing them unlearnedly and impiously. 

Therefore, lest an abundance of **Exorcisms** be desired any longer by those who long for the knowledge of them to dispel such a wretched and atrocious sickness, and to bruise and crush the audacity of demons, I have decided to gather into one and publish the many Exorcisms which I found scattered and dissipated in various places. My intent is that from this collection, **Exorcists** may be able to draw weapons whenever they please, with which they may hereafter boldly engage in hand-to-hand combat with demons and [drive] them out from the bodies which they most unjustly <term>possess</term><note>original: "obsident" — literally "besiege," suggesting the demon is an occupying force within the victim.</note>, with God [helping]...

<meta>catchword: helping-</meta>

<summary>In this preface, the author laments that the knowledge of exorcism has been lost to time, hidden by selfish practitioners, or corrupted by superstition. He justifies his work as a necessary collection of "weapons" for priests to combat demonic possession using established religious authority rather than folk magic.</summary>

<keywords>exorcism, spiritual warfare, demonic possession, church tradition, superstition, priest duties, medical metaphor</keywords>

<meta>This page concludes the author's preface. It transitions from a critique of negligent or superstitious exorcists to a formal dedication to the Cardinal of Bologna. The text concludes with a statement of purpose: to provide a practical tool for those suffering from spiritual afflictions.</meta>

# PREFACE.

<meta>The text continues from the previous page's discussion of those who fail to follow God's ways in exorcism.</meta>

*...so that with His help, they may cast them out. Why I should wish this work of mine (whatever its worth) to go forth marked by your most distinguished name, **Most Illustrious Cardinal**, I would indeed most gladly relate: were I not sufficiently aware of how much both your eyes and ears recoil from either hearing or reading your own praises (which are indeed extraordinary and nearly countless). For to you (to say nothing of other things)—that is, to your solicitude and your care—the **Christian Republic of Bologna** <term>Christian Republic of Bologna</term><note>original: "Bononia Christiana Respublica" — this refers to the city of Bologna, which at the time of writing in 1576 was a major legal and intellectual center within the Papal States.</note> owes every benefit it possesses. Therefore, if any utility should be derived from this labor of mine, it seemed right that it should be credited to you by right.*

*I am conscious of what I intended. As for what I have actually achieved, let others be the judge; yet I wish to testify to them this one thing: that I undertook this labor for no other reason than my hope that this work of mine will be useful to those who, in our times, are so miserably and ruinously tormented by the works of **Demons** and **Evildoers** <term>evildoers</term><note>original: "Maleficorum" — in this context, it refers specifically to practitioners of "maleficium" or harmful magic/witchcraft.</note>. Farewell, most noble Prince, and*
->*long may you live in happiness. Given at Bologna, on the Kalends of December* <note>The "Kalends" refers to the first day of the month in the Roman calendar; thus, December 1, 1576.</note> *1576.*<-

<summary>In this concluding section of the preface, the author dedicates his manual on exorcism to the Cardinal of Bologna, expressing hope that the work will provide relief to those suffering from demonic possession and harmful magic.</summary>
<keywords>dedication, Cardinal, Bologna, 1576, exorcism, demons, maleficium, spiritual warfare</keywords>

These verses praise Girolamo Menghi's expertise and introduce his two major works on exorcism: the *Flagellum Daemonum* (Whip of Demons) and the *Fustis Daemonum* (Club of Demons).</meta>

<page-num>289</page-num>
<header>EPIGRAM.</header>

# BY GIOVAN ANNIBALE BE-
## dulli, Jurist from Viadana. To the 
### Exorcists.

> Menghi has taught you the "Whip" <note>original: "flagellum" — a reference to Menghi's first book, *Flagellum Daemonum* (1577).</note> with wondrous art,
> By which the struck demon yields and seeks the depths.
> Now, revealing the secrets of Holy Scripture, he has fashioned
> The "Club," <note>original: "Fustem" — a reference to Menghi's second book, *Fustis Daemonum* (1584).</note> by which Satan, terrified, will surrender his arms.
> Therefore, let the enemy cease to fight against Menghi’s "Whip";
> He will be forced to retreat by the "Club."

# IN PRAISE OF THE AUTHOR.
## An Epigram by Andrea Pepoli of Cavallino.

> While you grant an easy way to drive the Stygian <term>Stygian</term><note>Referring to the River Styx in the underworld; here meaning hellish or demonic.</note> plagues 
> From the human body, Great Menghi:
> Henceforth the weapons, deceits, snares, fierce potions, and power 
> Of the demon will be worth nothing against your art.
> For in your wrath, you strike the back of Satan with the holy whip,
> Because you yourself snatch away his very strength.
> Wherefore the world will rightly decorate your name with honor,
> And will carry it forever with extraordinary praise.

# A DIALOGUE BY THE SAME AUTHOR.
## The Book and Alecto. <note>Alecto was one of the Furies in Greek and Roman mythology, personifying relentless anger. Here she represents the voice of the demonic realm.</note>

> **The Book:** Why do you rage, Alecto? Why does the wild palace of 
> Implacable Dis <term>Dis</term><note>Another name for Pluto, the god of the underworld.</note> thunder so? Where do you flee in your madness?
> Why does your treacherous Sister <note>referring to Megæra, another of the Furies.</note> tear her unkempt hair?
> And why does fierce Megæra roll such great fires?
> Where goes the wicked cohort of Satan? Why such great lightning?
> What do so many flames and so many thunders mean?
> **Alecto:** You, who alone have been our ruin, do you pretend?
> You, who alone prepare such dire scourges <note>original: "flagella"</note> for us?
> Alas, the grief! how it grows devouring; where will it reach, and from whence?
> To what end shall such ruin cease to fall upon our heads?

<page-num>293</page-num>

# THE SCOURGE
## OF DEMONS,
### AGAINST EVIL SPIRITS

->to be driven out from oppressed bodies,<-
->containing a most beautiful and highly necessary<-
->doctrine for Exorcists.<-
->By the Author, the Reverend Father Brother Girolamo Menghi<-
->of Viadana, of the Order of Friars Minor<-
->of the Regular Observance.<-
->Concerning the faith and preparation which Exorcists<-
->must have before they begin to<-
->adjure the demons.<-
->CHAPTER ONE.<-

<image-desc>An ornamental woodcut initial 'Q' set within a square border, decorated with floral and foliate patterns.</image-desc>
Whoever desires—rightly, easily, and without danger to their own salvation and that of others—to tread upon the infernal serpents <note>original: "calcare super serpentes infernales" — a reference to Psalm 91:13 and Luke 10:19, common motifs in exorcism literature regarding authority over evil spirits</note>, and to beware of all vain, illicit, and superstitious works; and to exercise the office of adjuring <term>adjurandi: the act of commanding a demon in the name of God or a higher power</term> demons existing in human bodies without sin; so that (with God’s favor) through this charity of exorcising, which among all works of mercy is the most laborious, he may deserve to reach eternal glory; he must, above all else, possess an undoubting faith in the mysteries of Jesus Christ and the Catholic faith. This is because the working of miracles (of which kind this work is) is ascribed to faith and is consistent with it.
<margin>Faith is necessary for Exorcists.</margin>
To this faith must also be added purity of conscience, achieved through sorrowful contrition for one's sins and pure sacramental confession;
<margin>
When the 
Priest's 
prayer be-
comes more 
effective.
</margin>
for even if the Priest, by his priestly power, prays in the person of the Church, his prayer is nevertheless more effective when it is pure on his own part, even though it derives its efficacy from the Church itself.

<sig>T 3</sig>
<meta>catchword: and also</meta>

<summary>This page serves as the formal opening of Girolamo Menghi's manual on exorcism, asserting that the practice is a "work of mercy" that requires the practitioner to have absolute faith and a clean conscience through confession.</summary>
<keywords>Girolamo Menghi, exorcism requirements, Catholic faith, spiritual preparation, power of the Church, works of mercy, priestly authority</keywords>

--- Page 10 ---
<meta>Continuity: This page continues the discussion from the end of Chapter One regarding the necessary preparation for an exorcist, specifically focusing on the purity of the priest’s conscience and the spiritual protection it provides against demonic taunts. It then transitions into Chapter Two, which outlines the requirements for the possessed person.</meta>

<page-num>294</page-num>
<header>M O S T   B E A U T I F U L   D O C T R I N E.</header>
<meta>catchword: so that under-</meta>

<margin>Sins forgiven through penance are hidden from the devil.</margin>
…and also the purity of that Priest, who, as a friend of God, deserves to be heard by Him; and also because sins which have been forgiven by God through true penance are hidden from the devil. Experience testifies that the devil cannot throw these sins back at the soul when he is compelled by divine power, especially when the Priest commands him to be silent. However, where a sin has not been forgiven by God, the demons—who know all things except the secret thoughts of the heart (as we stated in our vernacular <term>Compendio: likely referring to Menghi's "Compendio dell'arte esorcistica", a handbook written in the common Italian tongue rather than Latin</term>, which we published concerning the operations of demons and sorcerers, book 1, chapter 7)—will throw both true and false crimes back at the Priests. They do this to restrain the Priests from their command <note>original: "adjuratione" — the formal act of commanding the demon in God's name</note> by causing them confusion and shame. 
<margin>The cunning of demons against Exorcists.</margin>
Lest Exorcists cease this work for this reason, God does not grant this authority to the demons. 
<margin>The Exorcist must be humble.</margin>
The Exorcist must also possess the deepest humility, viewing himself in this work merely as an instrument of God; consequently, he must realize he can do nothing in this matter without His help. He should not seek praise from men for such deeds, but should rejoice and take pleasure in this work solely for the honor of Almighty God and the salvation of souls, lest he lose his merit and fall into sin. 
<margin>How much the humility of the Exorcist is feared by demons.</margin>
This is partly because demons are more easily put to flight when they are unable to endure the "odor of humility" <note>original: "odorem humilitatis" — a metaphorical spiritual fragrance that repels the prideful nature of fallen angels</note>, and partly because God bestows His gifts and grace upon the humble who use them well and in an orderly fashion. *Armed with all these things, let the Exorcist attack the devil with as much confidence as if he were about to perform some delightful task, for he shall see what the blessed God will do.*

---

->*Concerning the purity of conscience which the possessed person must have, and concerning the knowledge to be held by Exorcists. Chapter II.*<-

<margin>The possessed person must purge their own conscience.</margin>
The Exorcist must exhort the possessed person to purge their conscience with the greatest diligence. This is because their own prayer, and the prayers of those praying for them, will be more easily heard by God; and also because this demonic torment <note>original: "vexatio"</note> is frequently permitted to happen on account of sins (as we said in our Handbook, book 2, chapter 11, and book 3, chapter 6). And he must instruct the person...

<summary>This page concludes the spiritual requirements for the priest—emphasizing that confession hides one's past sins from demonic taunts—and begins Chapter Two, which requires the possessed person to repent so their prayers for deliverance may be effective.</summary>
<keywords>penance, confession, humility, demonic cunning, Girolamo Menghi, Compendio, spiritual protection, possessed person's duty</keywords>

--- Page 11 ---
<meta>Continuity: This page continues Chapter Two from page 294, transitioning from the internal spiritual preparation of the Exorcist to the practical diagnostic steps required to identify a true demonic possession in a patient. It then begins Chapter Three, discussing the use of physical objects in the rite.</meta>

<page-num>295</page-num>
->ON EVIL SPIRITS<-

...so that he might submit himself humbly to God and pray that, if it is beneficial, he may be freed from unclean spirits. Then, the Exorcist should attempt to determine if such a person is truly possessed by the devil or not, lest it happen that he fatigues himself in vain—to the detriment of divine honor and <margin>How to obtain certain knowledge of the demon's presence.</margin> priestly dignity. He will know with certainty that a spirit is present there when he perceives the possessed person speaking or knowing things that they otherwise could not have spoken or known; for example, things occurring at a distance or hidden secrets. He will know it as probable, however, when he sees the person trembling under his hand at the sacred words <note>original: "sacra verba" — referring to the specific formulas of the exorcism rite</note>, or performing contortions, or suffering pains, or making unusual and sudden movements or rages, or dreading and loathing divine things—such as the sacraments (especially confession and the Eucharist), the names of the Saints, and divine prayers. Therefore, from the beginning, the <margin>What things should be placed upon the possessed person against demons.</margin> Exorcist must, as much as he can, compel the devil to show these signs by secretly binding some sacred object upon the possessed person—such as relics of the Saints or a slip of paper containing the words of God. If the possessed person, unaware of this object, shows a strange reaction to its presence, it will be a sign of a diabolical presence. But take note, because of the detractors of this <margin>Why demons do not speak in Latin.</margin> art, that demons possessing human bodies very rarely speak Latin. They do this for this reason: namely, so that they might not be believed to be present, and also because they use the natural instruments <note>the physical vocal organs</note> of those whom they oppress. Therefore, a devil possessing an uneducated person <note>original: "idiotam" — in this context, a layperson or someone who has not studied the liberal arts</note> does not easily respond in Latin, <margin>Why demons do not respond in French.</margin> nor does one possessing an Italian easily respond in French, because a tongue accustomed to one mode of speaking is more obedient to it and better disposed to the movements pleasing to it. On this matter, see our *Compendium*, book 2, chapter 11, in its entirety.

### Whether physical things can act upon a spirit, where this matter is most excellently treated. Chapter III.

<margin>The devil is to be attacked with external things.</margin>
The Exorcist will be able to attack the devil possessing a human body with external things, such as holy water, incense, sulfur, rue <note>a bitter herb historically used for its strong scent and perceived medicinal properties</note>, and medicines, as well as other drinks, all of which must be exorcised and blessed.

### ->MOST EXCELLENT DOCTRINE<-
<meta>catchword: harmonies</meta>

<margin>Physical things can mitigate demonic distress, and how they act upon the Spirit.</margin>
But because many oppose this chapter for various reasons, it is necessary to know according to **Richard of Middleton** <note>original: "Ricardū de Mediavilla" — a 13th-century Franciscan theologian</note> in his *Quodlibeta* <note>a "Quodlibet" was a formal academic debate on "whatever" topic was proposed</note> 3, question 8, that even if herbs, harmonies <note>original: "armoniæ" — referring here to musical scales or modes</note>, and physical things cannot, by their own natural power, totally exclude the distress by which a devil torments a human—when this is permitted by God or good Angels—they can nevertheless mitigate that distress. Indeed, that distress can be so small that these external things might be able to exclude it entirely. However, they do this not by acting upon the demon himself—since he is a separate spirit <note>original: "spiritus separatus" — a spirit not naturally bound to a physical body</note> upon which no body can naturally act—but by acting upon the person being tormented by the demon. For every cause of limited power can produce a more intense effect upon matter that is well-disposed toward it than upon matter that is not; for according to **the Philosopher** <note>Aristotle</note> in the second book of *On the Soul* <note>original: "secundo de anima"</note>, the "acts of active agents are in a well-disposed recipient" <note>original: "actu activorum sint in patiente bene disposito"</note>.

<margin>The devil is an agent of limited power.</margin>
The devil, however, is an agent of limited power; therefore, he can cause a more intense torment in a person who is physically predisposed to that specific distress than in a person with a contrary disposition. For example, the devil can more intensely torment a person with a **melancholic passion** <note>In medieval medicine, an excess of "black bile," associated with sadness and depression</note> if they are already physically predisposed to it, than he could a person of a contrary disposition.

<margin>Physical things change the disposition of the body.</margin>
It is certain, moreover, that herbs, harmonies, and many other physical things can greatly change the disposition of the body, and consequently, the movement of the senses. This is evident with herbs, as some incline the mind toward joy and others toward sadness. I say this also of harmonies, as is clear from **the Philosopher** in the eighth book of the *Politics*, where he maintains that different musical harmonies provoke different passions in humans.

<margin>The opinion of Boethius regarding physical things.</margin>
**Boethius** <note>A Roman philosopher whose works on music and logic were standard in medieval education</note> relates this same thing in his *Music*; so does the author of *The Origin of the Sciences*, where, speaking of the utility of music, he says it is effective for curing various infirmities. For it can sometimes happen that the devil

<margin>Whether a small distress can be removed by physical things.</margin>
is not permitted to torment a man except with a distress so small that it might be totally removed by some strong physical disposition to the contrary. In such a case, certain herbs or harmonies can so dispose the human body to the contrary (as we said above) that the distress is totally removed. For instance, the devil might sometimes torment a man with the distress of sadness so weakly that, through certain herbs or harmonies,

<summary>This page explains that while physical objects like herbs and music cannot directly harm a demon, they can alleviate a possessed person's suffering by altering their physical temperament (such as reducing melancholy), thereby making the body a less receptive "target" for the devil's limited power.</summary>

### ->ON EVIL SPIRITS<-

<margin>Whether one can be freed from a demon by physical things.</margin>
...harmonies, which can effect the expansion and diffusion of the spirits <note>In medieval physiology, "spirits" were subtle vapors in the blood that governed emotion and movement; "expansion" here refers to lifting the spirits to counter the "contraction" of sadness</note>—which are movements contrary to sadness—to totally remove a person from that sadness. However, I do not see how herbs and other physical things could create such a state in a man that he could never be tormented by a demon when the torment is at its strongest; for herbs and harmonies (as we said above) cannot cause this state by their own natural power.

<margin>Harmful effects cannot happen without the help of demons.</margin>
We said this last part because of some people who try to claim that certain harmful effects <note>original: "maleficiales" — referring to *maleficia*, or works of harmful magic</note> could be produced through herbs and hidden causes alone, without the help of demons, relying only on the influence of the heavenly bodies <note>The author is refuting the idea that astrology or "natural magic" alone can explain the works of witches or sorcerers</note>, which they claim can impress power into physical things for physical effects just as much as the demons themselves can to produce such harmful effects.

<margin>When David played the lyre, Saul was relieved. The possessed are relieved by melody and physical things.</margin>
This is also proved by the authority of Holy Scripture. For in the First Book of Kings, chapter 16 <note>Modern 1 Samuel 16:23</note>, it is said of Saul that when he was vexed by an unclean spirit, and David played the lyre before him, he was relieved—though the spirit did not fully depart from him. Explaining this, **Lyra** <note>original: "Liranus" — Nicholas of Lyra (c. 1270–1349), a highly influential Franciscan biblical commentator</note> says: It seems reasonable to grant that through melody or other physical things, those afflicted by demons <note>original: "Energumeni" — a term for the possessed or those troubled by spirits</note> can bear their suffering more easily. This is because a physical thing can cause a state in the human body that makes it less subject to the demon's actions, and thus the suffering they cause is lessened and relieved.

<margin>Tobias is freed from a demon by the fish's organs.</margin>
And in the Book of Tobit, chapter 6, the Angel Raphael drove the demon away from Sarah and Tobias using the intestines of a fish, saying that if the liver of that fish is placed upon coals, its smoke drives out every kind of demon from both man and woman. But this was done with the help of prayer and three days of continence, along with the binding of the demon by the Angel.

<margin>Pope Alexander's opinion on physical things.</margin>
What more? Saint **Pope Alexander**, the first of this name, in his first epistle, commanded all priests by his own example to bless these things for the people, saying: "We bless water sprinkled with salt so that all who are sprinkled by it may be sanctified and purified, and we command all priests to do the same."

<margin>The virtues of water blessed by the Church.</margin>
For if the barrenness of the water was healed by the salt scattered by the prophet **Elisha** <note>original: "Helisæū" — referring to the miracle in 2 Kings 2:19-22</note>, how much more does water consecrated by divine prayers take away the barrenness of human affairs;

<margin>The Exorcist ought to irritate the demons, and why.</margin>

The reason for this is to prevent the possessed person from being deprived of their senses by the demon—which he frequently does so that the victim cannot attentively perceive the words of God, by which they might be moved to remorse and silently stirred to worship. It is also because the proud demon is provoked by these words and insults to depart from that place where he is struck by God through exorcisms and mocked by men with insults and mocking words. Nor 
<margin>Whether an Exorcist ought to inflict injury upon the devil.</margin>
does it stand in the way that we ought not to inflict injury upon or curse any creature; for in this, the Exorcist does not curse the devil as a creature of God out of hatred, but rather out of love for God, against whom the devil rebels, and out of charity for his neighbor. Since demons, established in the course of this life, are our adversaries, it is permitted for us to use words of insult against them so that they may be driven away from the neighbor whom they unjustly torment.

This is possible because, although they are all in agreement when it comes to harming humans, nonetheless <margin>Demons have enmities among themselves.</margin> they have enmities and battles among themselves; because of this, one will sometimes gladly punish another at our command.

## Concerning certain things to be avoided by Exorcists in both public and secret conjuration.

### Chapter V.

### *On the perseverance and patience to be maintained by Exorcists, and the reason why demons sometimes do not depart. Chapter VI.*

<margin>*The Exorcist must have the greatest patience.*</margin>
<margin>*God usually allows demons to remain in their stubbornness.*</margin>
<margin>*The "stings" by which demons are tortured.*</margin>
The Exorcist must have the greatest patience and perseverance in the <term>conjuration</term> <note>original: "conjuratione" — the formal act of swearing or commanding the spirit under oath to God</note> itself, so that he does not despair of victory if he sees the demons remaining in their stubbornness for a long time. To clarify this, it must be understood that the Priest’s commands for the devil to depart are actually the commands of God: for they are brought forth from the person of God. The devil would yield to them immediately and suddenly if God wished to use His omnipotent power—which He does not always do. Instead, He usually leaves the demon to his own will, whether he wishes to obey or not; just as He does with humans to whom God has commanded certain things. This is because God normally moves all things according to their own nature—meaning rational beings are moved with their freedom intact.

However, just as He applies "stings" to men so that they might want to obey—through preachers, adversities, and the like—so He also applies urgent stings to the evil spirits to force their obedience. These stings are the Exorcisms, by which the demons are as spiritually tortured as men are by physical lashes. Similarly, they use mocking words to assist in the liberation of the <term>possessed</term> <note>original: "obsessis" — those "besieged" or occupied by a demon</note>, as we mentioned above in chapter three. Thus, some demons are harder to expel than others because they are stronger in enduring insults and in despising the divine words. If, however, they do not eventually yield...

<meta>catchword: compelled-</meta>

<summary>This page advises exorcists to be cautious with relics to avoid demonic deception and explains that exorcism works like a "spiritual lash" rather than an instant miracle, as God respects the free will of even fallen spirits.</summary>
<keywords>exorcism protocol, relics, demonic cunning, spiritual torture, free will, patience, conjuration</keywords>

<page-num>303</page-num>
<header>AGAINST MALIGNANT SPIRITS</header>

God applies coercive power when He judges it opportune. Indeed, our God himself, who gave the power of adjuring <note>original: "adjurandi" — the act of commanding a spirit in the name of God</note> demons to Priests and the faithful, sometimes withdraws them from our obedience for a hidden cause: just as sometimes He does not wish that those commanded should manifest His name, either because it does not sound well, or because they might respond to questions asked when it is not expedient.

### On the Eucharist to be offered to the Possessed, contrary to the common opinion of the masses. Chapter VII.

<margin>The Eucharist must be given to the <term>Energumens [energumeni]</term> <note>original: "Energumenis" — a term used in the early Church for those troubled by unclean spirits</note>.</margin>
<margin>Testimony of Cassian regarding the common opinion.</margin>
The Exorcist ought to encourage the <term>obsessed [obsessum]</term> person to frequently receive the most sacred communion of the Eucharist. This is contrary to the common sentiment and opinion of the masses, who assert that those vexed by demons and the bewitched cannot be liberated if they receive communion while the demon is still upon them. How far this opinion is from the truth is clear through Cassian <note>John Cassian (c. 360–435), an influential monk whose "Conferences" are cited here</note> in Conference 7, chapter 30. Favoring our proposal, he says: "We do not remember that the holy communion was ever forbidden by our elders to those handed over to the spirits of wickedness; rather, if it were possible, they judged it should be imparted to them daily. It must be believed that this leads to the purification and protection of body and soul, and that when perceived by a person, it drives out that spirit which sits in his members or tries to hide in them, burning him with a kind of fire." 

<margin>Abbot Andronicus was freed from a demon.</margin>
"For in this way," Cassian continues, "we recently saw Abbot Andronicus cured. The enemy will insult the afflicted person when he sees him separated from the celestial medicine; indeed, he will attempt to afflict him more harshly and more frequently the further he senses him to be removed from the spiritual remedy." And if it is objected that he who wishes to receive the communion of the sacred body and blood of our Lord Jesus Christ must prove himself, so that he may eat and drink worthily from that bread and cup—which the obsessed cannot do, since they lack the use of reason—

<margin>The type of use of reason that the possessed must have.</margin>
to this objection Saint Thomas <note>Thomas Aquinas (1225–1274), the preeminent scholastic theologian</note> responds in the third part [of the Summa], question 80, saying: "Some are said not to have the use of reason in a double..."

<meta>catchword: duplici- (doubly)</meta>

<summary>This page argues that, contrary to popular belief, victims of demonic possession should receive the Eucharist frequently, as the holy sacrament acts as a "celestial medicine" that can drive out spirits. It cites historical authorities like John Cassian and Thomas Aquinas to support the practice of giving communion to the "obsessed."</summary>
<keywords>Eucharist, Holy Communion, possession, Energumens, John Cassian, Thomas Aquinas, Abbot Andronicus, spiritual remedy</keywords>

--- Page 20 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of Chapter VII regarding whether the Holy Eucharist should be administered to those suffering from demonic possession or witchcraft, concluding with practical advice on searching for physical charms and transitioning into Chapter VIII regarding the sacred names of God.</meta>

<page-num>384</page-num>
<header>MOST BEAUTIFUL DOCTRINE</header>
<meta>catchword: And because</meta>

<margin>What kind of devotion to the Eucharist is required in the obsessed.</margin>
...in two ways. In one way, because they have a weak use of reason, much like someone who sees poorly is sometimes called "blind." Because such people are able to conceive of some devotion toward this sacrament, the sacrament must not be denied to them. In another way, some are said to have no use of reason because they have remained in that state from birth; for these, the sacrament is not to be granted, as no devotion toward it could have existed in them beforehand. Alternatively, they may not have always lacked the use of reason; in this case, if they previously showed devotion toward the sacrament when they were of sound mind, the sacrament should be given to them—unless there is a fear of the danger of vomiting <note>original: "periculum vomitus" — a standard liturgical concern that the consecrated host might be accidentally desecrated if the possessed person cannot physically keep it down</note>.

<margin>Communion is not to be denied to <term>Energumens</term> <note>original: "Energumenis" — those troubled by spirits</note>.</margin>
And although the text there speaks of the sick, nevertheless in his fourth book, distinction nine <note>likely referring to Peter Lombard’s "Sentences," a standard medieval theological textbook</note>, he says that communion is not to be denied to demoniacs unless it is certain they are being tortured by a demon as punishment for a specific crime. From this it is clear that even if some are possessed by a demon because of their crimes, as long as they have "lucid intervals" and the use of reason, and later feel contrition for their sins and make a proper confession, they are absolved before God. Such people are by no means to be separated from the communion of the most divine sacrament of the Eucharist.

<margin>Things applied to the <term>Energumens</term> are to be blessed.</margin>
The Exorcist ought also to exorcise and bless all those things which are to be applied to the use of the <term>bewitched</term> <note>original: "maleficiati"</note> or the demoniac, such as food, soups, ointments, and the like, as well as those things which contribute to the adjuration <note>the formal command given to a spirit to depart</note> of the demons existing in the human body.

<margin>Instruments of witchcraft are to be sought.</margin>
Then, he should make a diligent search through every corner, in the bedding, the mattresses, under the threshold of the door, and in all places of the house, to see if by chance any instruments of witchcraft <note>original: "instrumenta maleficii" — physical objects like knotted hair, bones, or charms believed to bind a curse to a person</note> can be found; if found, they are to be cast into the fire immediately. It is also expedient to renew all the bedding and clothing, and even to change one's habitation and house, as this often contributes to the liberation of the obsessed person.

### *On the explanation of certain Hebrew and Greek names of God, which are placed here and there in the Exorcisms. Chapter VIII.*

<margin>Beware of unknown names.</margin>
The exorcist must be careful not to use any unknown names <note>referring to "barbarous" or magical-sounding words whose meaning is not clear</note>, since according to St. Chrysostom <note>John Chrysostom (c. 347–407), an Early Church Father known for his eloquence</note>, such names are always to be feared. And because...

It also provides practical instructions for exorcists to bless the victim's food and search the home for physical charms or "instruments of witchcraft."</summary>

It addresses potential concerns that these names might be superstitious by providing an authoritative list based on the writings of Saint Jerome.</meta>

<page-num>305</page-num>

### ON EVIL SPIRITS
V
<meta>catchword: Marvellous</meta>

<margin>Holy Names explained by Saint Jerome.</margin>
Since there are many names in this collection of exorcisms of ours that are unknown to the simple <note>original: "pusillis" — referring here to those with little training or the "little ones" in faith</note>, and yet they are the most holy names of God, I have decided to insert an explanation of them here. I do this so that no one’s mind is left in a state of confusion, and so that every Exorcist may use these conjurations securely and without doubt whenever he pleases. It should be known, therefore (according to Saint Jerome in his letter to Marcella, in the third volume of his *Epistles*), that the first name of God is **El**, which the Seventy <note>The "Septuagint," the ancient Greek translation of the Hebrew Bible</note> interpret as "the Strong God." The second and third are **Eloym** and **Eloa**, which also mean "God." The fourth is **Sabaoth**, which the Seventy translated as "of powers" <note>original: "virtutum"</note>, but Aquila <note>A 2nd-century translator of the Hebrew Bible into Greek</note> translated as "of hosts." The fifth is **Eljon**, which we call "the Most High." The sixth is **Esercheje** <note>original: "Esercheje," a Latinization of the Hebrew *Ehyeh asher Ehyeh*</note>, which is read in Exodus as "He who is, sent me." The seventh is **Adonay**, which we generally call "the Lord." The eighth is **Ya**, which is applied only to God, and it also sounds as the final syllable in *Alleluia*. The ninth is the **Tetragrammaton**, which the Hebrews considered the **Jehovah**, that is, the ineffable name. The tenth is **Saday**, which among the Hebrews is used for the "God of Heaven" according to the translation of Aquila; we, however, can understand it as "the Robust One," or the one "sufficient for accomplishing all things." Thus writes Saint

<margin>Names of God which are criticized without cause.</margin>
<margin>The error of Paulus Ghirlandus regarding the names of God.</margin>
Jerome in the place cited above. Besides these, there are many other unknown names of God placed there which are rejected by some as if they were superstitious and to be condemned. However, if these people weighed them well, they would not be ignorant of the fact that they are holy names. Such names include **Aglos, Otheos, Ischiros, Athanatos** <note>Greek: "Holy, God, Strong, Immortal," used in the Trisagion hymn</note>. Regarding these, Paulus Ghirlandus <note>Paolo Grillandi, a 16th-century Italian jurist famous for his writings on witchcraft</note> says in his treatise *On Sorceries* that he often snatched these names from the hands of sorcerers. He claims they mean nothing more than "God of Acharon, the immortal God," and he asserts it is clear that this name **Acharon** <note>Possibly a corruption of Acheron, a river in the underworld, or a misreading of a Hebrew term</note> is a name for Satan or Beelzebub. How far this explanation stands from the truth can be seen by the fact that the Holy Catholic and Roman Church uses these very names on the *Parasceve* <note>The traditional liturgical name for Good Friday</note> to seek God's pardon for the sins of the whole world. If these words were vain and superstitious, not only would the Church not use them, but they would be condemned by her.

<summary>This page lists and explains ten holy names of God derived from Saint Jerome's scholarship to provide exorcists with a theological defense for their use. It specifically refutes the claims of the jurist Paolo Grillandi, who mistakenly identified certain Greek liturgical names as demonic or superstitious.</summary>

<page-num>306</page-num>
<header>MOST BEAUTIFUL DOCTRINE</header>
<meta>catchword: most-wise</meta>

<margin>*Sother and Jehovah are explained.*</margin>
I wonder, therefore, that such an upright, prudent, and learned man failed to notice this matter, since these things are clear to the eyes of all, and especially to the faithful. Furthermore, there are certain other names there, not known to everyone, placed here and there, such as **Sother**, which is a Greek name and means exactly the same as "Savior" <note>original: "Salvator"</note> among us; and among the Hebrews, **Jehovah**, which is for them the ineffable <note>unpronounceable or too sacred to be spoken</note> name. **Jehovah** is the great name of God, the interpretation of which you can see in the work of Galatinus <note>Pietro Colonna Galatino (c. 1460–1540), a Franciscan friar who wrote "De Arcanis Catholicae Veritatis" (On Secrets of Catholic Truth), defending Christian doctrine through Jewish sources.</note> against the Hebrews. It is also found in the popular book by the Reverend Father Brother Archangelo del Pozzo <note>original: "Archangeli Putei"</note>, which he published regarding the name of **Jesus**.

<margin>*Agla and its explanation.*</margin>
**Agla** is a Hebrew name, and it is placed among the great names of God; among the Hebrews it means exactly the same as "Thou art God, powerful forever" <note>original: "tu es Deus potens in æternum." This is a common Hebrew acronym: *Atah Gibor Le-olam Adonai*.</note> among us, according to the same Galatinus in the second book he published on the divine names against the Hebrews.

<margin>*Homousion is explained.*</margin>
**Homousion** is a Greek name and means the same as "consubstantial" <note>original: "consubstantialis" — sharing the same substance or essence</note>, which is attributed to Christ, who is consubstantial with the Father and the Holy Spirit. **Eheye** <note>original: "Eheye," from the Hebrew *Ehyeh*</note> is a Hebrew name and signifies the most simple essence of the divinity of God, according to the same Reverend Father Brother Archangelo del Pozzo.

---

### *On the explanation of certain Latin names of God, which are placed here and there in the Exorcisms by the author. Chapter IX.*

<margin>*The Author satisfies the desire of some.*</margin>
Because there are certain names of God in the exorcisms of the *Scourge* <note>original: "Flagelli," referring to the *Flagellum Daemonum* (Scourge of Demons), a famous exorcism manual by Girolamo Menghi.</note> placed in various locations, extracted from Holy Scripture or from the Holy Doctors—the sources of which some people have desired to have identified—I have done so so that every Exorcist may be able to give an account of them for himself, and so that a proper place may be assigned to each name, allowing him to use them securely whenever he wishes. **Alpha. Omega. First. Last. Beginning. End.** These are found in Revelation, chapter 1: *I am Alpha and Omega, the First and the Last, the Beginning and the End.* **Messiah. Christ.** John 4: *I know that the Messiah is coming, who is called Christ.* **Jesus.** Luke 2: *His name was called Jesus.* **King.** John 18: *You say that I am a King.* And of the Church, most-wise... <note>The text cuts off here at the catchword "sapissi-" for the next page.</note>

# AGAINST MALIGNANT SPIRITS
### V 2

<margin>King: often used by the Church.</margin>
The Church very frequently uses this name, **King**, such as: "O Christ, most merciful King" <note>original: "Rex Christe clementissime"</note>; "King and maker of the ages" <note>original: "Rex atque factor temporum"</note>; "Eternal King, Lord"; "When that most mighty King"; "O eternal King most high"; "The King of nations poured out"; and "King of glorious martyrs." **Emmanuel** and **Lawgiver**: in Isaiah 7 it says, "Behold, a virgin shall conceive and bear a son, and his name shall be called Emmanuel." And the Church, in the first Great O Antiphon <note>The "O Antiphons" are seven short prayers sung in the days leading up to Christmas, each addressing Christ by a biblical title.</note>, sings of Christ: "O Emmanuel, our King and Lawgiver." **Light**: in John 8, "I am the light of the world."

<margin>Lord and Master are noted in the Scriptures.</margin>
**Father**: in Matthew 6, "When you pray, say: Our Father." **Master** and **Lord**: in John 13, "You call me Master and Lord, and you say well, for so I am." **Beginning**: in John 8, "The Beginning, who also speaks to you." **Image**: Paul to the Colossians, chapter 1, "Who is the image of the invisible God." **Door**: in John 10, "I am the Door." **Way, Truth, and Life**: in John 14, "I am the way, the truth, and the life." **Prophet**: in Luke 7, "A great prophet has risen among us." **Rock**: in 1 Corinthians 10, "And the rock was Christ." **Vine**: in John 15, "I am the true vine." **Key**: in Isaiah 22 and Revelation 3; and the Church also calls Christ this in the fourth Great O Antiphon. **Power** <note>original: "Virtus," which can mean virtue, power, or strength.</note>: in Luke 1, "The power of the Most High shall overshadow you." **Firstborn**: in Luke 2, "She brought forth her firstborn son."

<margin>Lamb and sheep are expressed.</margin>
**Lamb**: in Isaiah 16 <note>original: "Esa 26," likely a misprint or variant citation for Isaiah 16:1 or similar.</note>, "Send forth the lamb, O Lord, the ruler of the earth." Also in chapter 53, "As a lamb before the shearer." In Jeremiah 11 and 51, "As a gentle lamb led to the slaughter." **Sheep**: in Isaiah 53, "Like a sheep he shall be led to the slaughter." **Bridegroom**: in Psalm 18, "As a bridegroom coming out of his chamber." **Creator**: in Genesis 1, "In the beginning God created the heaven and the earth." The Church sings: "God, the Creator of all," "Come, Holy Spirit, Creator," "O best Creator of things," and "O best Creator of light." **Redeemer**: the Church sings many times, "O Christ, Redeemer of all." **Shepherd**: in John 10 <note>original: "Jo. 12"</note>, "I am the good shepherd." **Teacher**: in Luke 5, "Teacher, we have labored all night and caught nothing."

<margin>Splendor is declared.</margin>
**Radiance** and **Substance**: in Hebrews 1, "Who, being the radiance of his glory and the figure of his substance." The Church sings: "You are the light, you are the radiance of the Father," and "To you, O Christ, radiance of the Father." **Judge**: in 2 Timothy 4, "The Lord, the just judge, will render to me on that day." **Wisdom**: in Proverbs 9, "Wisdom has built herself a house." In 1 Corinthians 1, "Who has become for us wisdom." And the Church, in the first Great O Antiphon, sings of Christ: "O Wisdom, who came out of the mouth of the Most High." **Sun**: in Malachi 4, "To you who fear my name..."

# MOST BEAUTIFUL DOCTRINE
### V 2

<margin>
Sun: why Christ is so called.
</margin>
...when the **Sun of Justice** <note>original: "Sol justitiæ"</note> arose. And the Church, speaking to the Blessed Virgin Mary, says: "For from you arose the **Sun of Justice**." **Morning Star** (Revelation 22): "I am the root of David, and the bright and morning star." **Flower** (Isaiah 11): "And a flower shall rise from his root." **Priest** (The Church): "The Lord Christ is a priest forever." Called **High Priest** <note>original: "Pontifex"</note> by God according to the order of Melchizedek. **Love** (1 John 4): "God is love." The Church says: "**Living Fountain**, **Fire**, **Love**." **Only-begotten** (John 1/3): "For God so loved the world, that he gave his only-begotten son."

<margin>
The Church uses "Paraclete."
</margin>

The Church sings: "You who are called Paraclete." **Mediator** (1 Timothy 2): "One mediator between men, the man Christ Jesus." **God**, **Eternal**, **Almighty**: The Church very often uses these names. **Power** <note>original: "Virtus"</note> (1 Corinthians 1): "But we preach Christ, the power of God"; and in the same place: "To those who are being saved, he is the power of God to us." **Bread** (John 6): "I am the living bread." **Salvation**: The Church says: "You have come as the salvation of the world." **Lion** (Revelation 5): "The Lion of the tribe of Judah has conquered." **Light** (Luke 2): "For the revelation of the Gentiles." **Mouth** (Isaiah 25): "For the mouth of the Lord has spoken." **Word** (John 1): "In the beginning was the Word. And the Word was made flesh."

"Holy" is found in the Scriptures.
</margin>

(Isaiah 58): "Because I, the Lord your God, am merciful." (Jonah 4): "Because you are a gracious and merciful God." (James 5): "You have seen the end of the Lord, that the Lord is merciful." **Immortal** (1 Timothy 1): "To the King of ages, immortal." The Church sings: "Holy, Strong, and Immortal." **Peaceful King**: The Church sings: "The peaceful King is magnified." **Rising Sun** <note>original: "Oriens," meaning the dawn or the East.</note>: The Church, in the fifth Great O Antiphon <note>Part of the "O Antiphons" sung in the days before Christmas.</note>, calls upon Christ saying: "O Rising Sun, splendor of eternal light, and Sun of justice, and life."

<margin>
God is called the Supreme Good.
</margin>
**Goodness**: The Church sings: "O Christ, true light, goodness." **Supreme Good**: The Master of Sentences <note>Peter Lombard (c. 1100–1160), whose 'Sentences' was the primary theology textbook of the Middle Ages.</note> says in Book 2, first distinction: "God made the rational creature so that it might understand the supreme good, and in understanding, love it; in loving, possess it; and in possessing, enjoy it." **Hope**...

## Chapter 10: On the explanation of certain names of the blessed virgin Mary, which are placed scattered throughout the Exorcisms of the Scourge.

<margin>
Virgin: Mary is often named so.
</margin>
<margin>
Dawn: Mary is named so in the Scriptures.
</margin>
<margin>
Gate, Star: Mary is called these.
</margin>
Because it was desired by many that a place in the Scripture or the Saints be assigned to every name of the blessed virgin Mary—where the names written below were excerpted from—I have therefore decided to insert the aforementioned locations here, so that Exorcists might be rendered more certain. The first name placed here, therefore, is **Virgin**. Isaiah 7: "Behold, a virgin shall conceive" <note>original: "Ecce virgo concipiet"</note>. The Church: "The virgin Mary has been taken up," and "O clement, O pious, O sweet virgin." **Mary**. Luke 1: "Hail Mary, full of grace." Luke 2: "Mary kept all these words"; and very often in the four Gospels she is called the Mother of our Lord Jesus Christ. **Queen**. The Church: "Queen of heaven, rejoice" <note>The 'Regina Caeli' chant.</note>. "Hail, Queen of the heavens." **Glorious**. **Lady** <note>original: "Domina"</note>. Luke 1: "Behold the handmaid of the Lord." **Garden**. **Fountain**. Song of Songs 4: "An enclosed garden, a sealed fountain" <note>original: "Ortus conclusus, fons signatus." These are traditional symbols of Mary's virginity.</note>. **Well**. In the same place: "A well of living waters." **Moon**. **Sun**. Song of Songs 6: "Fair as the moon, bright as the sun." **Gate**. Ezekiel 44: "This gate shall be closed to sin." The Church: "Happy gate of heaven." **Blessed**. Luke 1: "And blessed are you who believed." The Church: "Blessed are you, Virgin Mary, who carried the Lord, the Creator of the world." **Pious** <note>original: "Pia," meaning merciful or dutiful.</note>. The Church: "O clement, O pious, O sweet virgin Mary." **The Bush** <note>original: "Rubus"</note>. The Church: "The bush which Moses had seen unburnt" <note>A reference to the Burning Bush of Exodus 3, interpreted as a symbol of Mary bearing God without losing her virginity.</note>. **Door**. The Church: "You are the door of the high King, and the glittering gate of light." **Star**. The Church.

<meta>This page continues the list of titles for the Virgin Mary begun on page 309. The previous page established the scriptural basis for calling her 'Virgin,' 'Dawn,' and 'Star.' This page moves into more complex metaphorical titles used in exorcism and liturgy, such as 'Fleece' and 'Ladder,' citing medieval authorities like Bernard of Clairvaux and Bernardino de Bustis to authorize these names for use against demons.</meta>

->### BEAUTIFUL DOCTRINE<-

The Church: *Hail, Star of the Sea.* **Tower**. Song of Solomon 7: "Your neck is like a tower of ivory." **Beautiful**. Song of Solomon 6: "You are beautiful, my beloved." The Church: "You are beautiful and graceful." **Beloved**. **Fair**. Song of Solomon 6 <note>Likely referring to Song of Solomon 2:10</note>: "Arise, hasten, my beloved, my fair one." **Lovely**. The Church: "You have been made lovely and sweet." **Fleece**. Saint Bernard in his 8th Sermon on the Assumption of the Blessed Virgin Mary:

<margin>
Fleece: the Blessed Virgin Mary is called this.
</margin>
Mary (he says) is indeed the fleece, the medium between Christ and the Church. **Mother**. The Church: "Blessed mother and untouched glorious virgin," and "Mary, mother of grace." **Nourishing** <note>original: "Alma"</note>. The Church: *Nourishing Mother of the Redeemer.* **Flower**. Song of Solomon 2: "I am the flower of the field." **God-bearer** <note>original: "Theotokos"</note>. Thus Saint John Damascene, in Book 3, Chapter 12 of *On the Orthodox Faith*, names the Blessed Virgin Mary to counter the audacity of the impious Nestorius, who dared to deny that she was the Mother of God.

<margin>
Tabernacle: why the Blessed Mary is called this.
</margin>
**Tabernacle**. Thus Jean Gerson in his 8th treatise *On the Song of Mary* <note>A commentary on the Magnificat</note> names her, because Christ dwelt within her. **Ark**. Thus the Blessed Virgin Mary is called by Saint John Damascene in his first sermon during the Octave of the Assumption of the same Blessed Virgin Mary; he says the same in his sermon for the fourth day. **Bridal Chamber**. The Church, on the Feast of the Purification of Mary, sings of her: "Adorn your bridal chamber."

<margin>
The Hall: Mary our Lady is so named.
</margin>
**Hall** <note>or Court; original: "Aula"</note>. Saint Fulgentius calls the womb of the Blessed Virgin Mary by this name in his sermon on Saint Stephen the Protomartyr. **Cloud**. Isaiah 45: "Drop down dew, ye heavens, from above, and let the clouds rain down the Just One"; and Ezekiel 32: "I will cover the sun with a cloud." For thus the Blessed Virgin Mary is named by Bernardino de Bustis in his *Mariale*, part 9, principal section, Sermon 3, part 5 of the same sermon under the letter D. **Pearl**. The Mother of Christ, Mary, is thus interpreted by the aforementioned Doctor <note>Bernardino de Bustis</note> in the cited book, part 3, principal section, Sermon 1, part 1 of the same sermon under the letter B.

<margin>
The Ladder: Saint Mary is so named.
</margin>
**Ladder**. Thus the Blessed Virgin Mary is named by Saint John Damascene in a certain sermon on that passage of Genesis 28: "Jacob saw a ladder," using these words: "The Virgin Mary is the intellectual ladder." And Saint Bernard asserts the same in these words: "The humility of Mary," he says, "became the heavenly ladder through which God descended to earth." And elsewhere: "O Virgin, sublime ladder, who raises your holy peak into the heights, even to Him sitting on the throne, even to the Lord of Majesty; upon which ladder, surely, the Lord leaned." These things are from
Bustis <note>The catchword "Bufto" refers to the author Bernardino de Bustis.</note>

<margin>
House: why she is called the Mother of God.

In the aforementioned book by Bustis <note>referring to Bernardino de Bustis, a 15th-century Franciscan theologian</note>, in the ninth principal part, the second Sermon, the second part of that same Sermon, under the letter D: She is called **House**. ## **Whether Exorcists may use scrolls: and concerning the rules by which this can be done lawfully.**
### Chapter XI.

<margin>
Exorcists may use scrolls.
</margin>
<margin>
The glory of God must be the end of our works.
</margin>
<margin>
The rites of the Church are to be kept in human works.
</margin>
<margin>
Our works should be conformed to the custom of the Churches.
</margin>
The Exorcist will be able to use certain scrolls <note>term: <term>schedulis</term>—slips of paper or parchment containing sacred names or scripture, sometimes worn or applied to the body</note> containing certain holy names and words, and apply them to those possessed by demons <note>original: "Energumenis"</note> to remove their affliction. For the clarification of this, it must be noted that five rules are given for knowing whether these "charms" <note>original: "brevia"</note> are lawful or superstitious. The first rule is that the glory of God must be the principal end which we ought to seek in all things; namely, that one attends to whether the work is for the glory of God, and that in that work the man gives glory principally to God: and through that work itself, the mind of the man is subjected to God, according to that passage in 1 Corinthians 10: *Whether you eat or drink, or whatever else you do, do all for the glory of God.* The second rule is that one should see whether the work is something that pertains to some bodily abstinence or to the exercise of the body, and whether it restrains concupiscence <note>strong desire or lust</note> in a way that is virtuously due, according to the rite of the Church or according to moral doctrine, following the Apostle in Romans 12: *Let your service be reasonable.* The third rule is that one should see whether the work is according to the state of the Universal Church, or according to the testimony of Holy Scripture, or at least...

->MOST BEAUTIFUL DOCTRINE<-
312

<margin>The customs of the Church ought to be kept.</margin>
...according to the rite of a particular Church, or by general custom, which according to Augustine <note>St. Augustine of Hippo (354–430 AD), an influential theologian who often wrote on Church tradition.</note> is to be held as law. Concerning this, the Blessed Gregory wrote to Augustine, the Bishop of the English <note>referring to St. Gregory the Great’s letter to Augustine of Canterbury regarding the establishment of the Church in England</note>, saying: "There are diverse customs of the Church in the celebration of the Mass; it pleases me," he said, "that whether in the Roman, the Gallican, or in whichever Church you have found something that might better please Almighty God, you should choose it with care." And Saint Thomas <note>Thomas Aquinas</note> says: "The customs of the Church in divine worship in no way oppose divine truth."

<margin>Natural property is necessary in good works.</margin>
The fourth rule is that it should be examined whether the work employed or performed has a **natural property** <note>original: "naturalem proprietatem"; the inherent physical capacity of a thing to produce a specific effect</note> to achieve the expected effect, or not. Regarding this, Physicians, Doctors, and Astronomers—insofar as they are such—can judge many things, as can Theologians.

<margin>Scandal must be avoided in our good works.</margin>
The fifth rule is that it be considered whether the work is meritorious or not: whether the thing being done offers an occasion for any **scandal** <note>original: "scandali"; in this context, an action that leads others into sin or spiritual confusion</note>, or whether it might be a cause of ruin for one's neighbor. For in that case, even if the act were not superstitious, it should nonetheless be abandoned, or postponed, or done secretly and without scandal. Whatever, therefore, is contrary to any of the aforementioned rules is to be feared as superstitious or sinful.

<margin>Ceremonial rites are prohibited in the New Testament.</margin>
Because of the first rule, it is unlawful in the New Testament to practice the ceremonial or judicial laws of the Old Testament, unless they have been newly instituted by the Catholic and Roman Church. Because of the same rule, and because of the second rule, those who vow not to comb their hair on Saturday, or to fast on Sunday as if it were a "better" day, and similar things, act foolishly—unless they vow to fast on Saturday out of some pure devotion to the honor of the Most Blessed Virgin Mary. Because of the third rule, those act superstitiously who do not conform to the community in the rites of any particular church, such as in processions and the like, unless they are excused for some other reason.

<margin>Characters and vain observations must be avoided.</margin>
Because of the fourth rule, those act superstitiously who use **characters** <term>caracteres</term> <note>occult symbols or sigils believed to have magical power</note>, or unknown or suspect names, or methods of binding <note>amulets or physical charms tied to the body</note> that contribute nothing naturally to the expected effect. This is because these things have no **natural activity** <note>inherent physical power</note> in themselves, since they are merely artificial. The same must be said of images made by astronomers <note>astrological talismans</note>, and rings, and stones...

<summary>This page outlines five theological rules to distinguish between lawful religious practices and superstition, emphasizing Church tradition, natural law, and the avoidance of "scandal." It specifically warns against using occult symbols, astrological talismans, and outdated Old Testament rituals.</summary>

### AGAINST MALIGNANT SPIRITS

->313<-

with their artificial stones. Similarly, those who believe that the influences of the heavens or the stars necessitate <note>compel or force someone to act without free will</note> anyone toward any vice. <margin>Certain blessings are to be avoided on account of scandal.</margin> Regarding the fifth rule <note>Referring to the rule of avoiding "scandal," or actions that might lead others into spiritual error</note>, if a matter were notably scandalous, even certain blessings which are performed by laypeople through devout words over the sick, without evil rites, should not be performed publicly, lest an occasion of scandal be given. <margin>What a lawful blessing ought to be like.</margin> Therefore, he who desires to lawfully exorcise the sick must, beyond the rules mentioned above, observe seven other conditions set down by **St. Thomas** <note>Thomas Aquinas (1225–1274), a central philosopher and theologian for the Catholic Church</note> in the *Secunda Secundae*, question 96. When these conditions are present, the blessing will be lawful; if it is performed in the manner of an adjuration <note>a solemn command or appeal</note> through the power of the divine name, or the blood of Christ, or another sacred thing, such words are called a **lawful Exorcism**, and those who perform them may be called lawful **Exorcists**.

The **first condition**, therefore, is that he see to it that the words contain nothing that pertains to an express or tacit invocation of demons. <margin>Unknown names are to be dreaded.</margin> The **second condition** is that the Exorcisms do not include unknown names; for such things, according to **Chrysostom** <note>St. John Chrysostom (c. 347–407), an early Church Father known for his preaching</note>, are to be dreaded lest some superstition lie hidden within them. The **third** is that the substance of the words contains no falsehood; for in that case, an effect cannot be expected from God, since He Himself cannot be a witness to any falsehood. In this, certain old women often fail with their charms <note>original: "carminibus"; rhythmic chants or songs used in folk magic</note>, which include foolish and even false things. The **fourth** is that no vain things be brought in, nor characters <note>symbols, sigils, or occult signs</note> inscribed, except for the sign of the Cross. <margin>What kind of intention should be held in human works.</margin> The **fifth** is that hope should not be placed in the manner of writing, or any such vanity that has nothing to do with the matter or with divine reverence; otherwise, the whole thing is judged entirely superstitious. The **sixth** is that in the citation and utterance of divine words or Holy Scripture, regard should only be had for the sacred words themselves and their meaning; let there also be reverence for the power of God, from whom one awaits the effect, or for the relics of the saints, from whom something is expected secondarily, though primarily from God. <margin>The effect of our works is to be left to the divine will.</margin> The **seventh** is that the expected effect be left to the divine will and good pleasure, for He knows what should be granted more or less to the petitioner. Therefore, with these conditions observed, it is lawful to exorcise the sick who are **possessed** <note>original: "Energumenos"; those troubled by spirits</note>, and to hang amulets <note>original: "brevia"; small written scrolls or slips of paper containing sacred text</note> around their necks, or to carry them with one.

<center>AGAINST MALIGNANT SPIRITS</center>

## On foreknowing the deceits of demons and applying remedies. Chapter XII.

<margin>Demons seek to hide within possessed bodies.</margin>
An **Exorcist** must be diligent in recognizing and guarding against the deceits of demons residing in possessed bodies. While these tricks are nearly infinite, I will recount some here. It should be noted that, in most cases, the devil seeks to hide as much as he can and refrain from speaking or trembling. This is done so he is not discovered, or to appear strong and fearless before the **Priest**, causing the Priest to have less hope of victory over him. If he is forced to tremble by the power of divine words, he takes care not to speak internally to the mind of the possessed person. If he is forced to speak internally, he takes care that the possessed person does not speak on his behalf. Therefore, the Priest should advise the possessed person to tell him whatever the enemy says internally; he should also frequently adjure <note>To "adjure" is to command solemnly under oath or the threat of a curse, usually by the name of God</note> the enemy to answer the questions asked.

<margin>Demons seek to distract the mind of the possessed.</margin>
When he is forced to speak externally, he seeks, as much as he can, to distract the mind of the possessed person from the words being read, as well as his own mind. He also seeks to disturb the attention and devotion of the Priest, whether through frivolous words, groans, shouts, or by inciting bystanders to engage in trifles and similar things.

<margin>The trickery of demons to be avoided by the Exorcist.</margin>
Therefore, the Priest, having recognized this malice, should strive for the opposite and always command the enemy in the power of God to desist from this or that action, etc.

<margin>Demons send lustful fantasies.</margin>
Sometimes, when the woman being exorcised is quite young, the demon pretends to be distressed when the Priest touches her face. He does this so that the Priest might touch her more firmly, thereby inducing <term>voluptuous fantasies</term> <note>original: "voluptuosa fantasmata"; refers here to lustful or sensual mental images intended to tempt both the victim and the priest</note> in the woman. For the same reason, he pretends to be distressed when the Priest, while speaking, comes too close to her mouth, so that he might bring his face even closer to hers. Therefore, let the Priest be modest and cautious in his eyes, hands, and all his movements—not only for his own sake but also for the sake of the possessed woman—and all the more so because he cannot know her inner thoughts.

<margin>Let the Exorcist be cautious of the devil’s craftiness.</margin>
Sometimes, when asked for the name of the leader, he gives his own name but says he cannot give the names of his other companions because God does not wish it, or because the names are foul. Although this is sometimes true, it is frequently false. And therefore, [the Priest] should strive...

<meta>The catchword at the bottom, "Sacer," indicates the next page begins with "Sacerdos" (The Priest).</meta>

<margin>Notable doctrine for **Exorcists**.</margin>
A **Priest** should compel the truth through a formal command. If he cannot obtain it, let him command the principal demon to give way and call another to speak, ordering him to reveal his name; and lest he fear to do this out of dread for his superior, let the Priest command the superior not to forbid him. But if he still cannot obtain the truth in this way, then the Priest himself should impose ridiculous names <note>original: *nomina irrisiva*; this was a common tactic in historical exorcism to humiliate the demon and break its pride by assigning it lowly or absurd titles.</note> upon the other spirits,

<margin>Ridiculous names to be imposed on demons by **Exorcists**.</margin>
for instance, by saying: let the first after the principal be called the "drudge" <note>original: *fachinus*; a porter or menial laborer</note>; the second, the "baker" <note>original: *pistor*</note>; the third, the "cook of Acheron" <note>original: *cocus Acherontis*; Acheron is one of the rivers of the underworld in classical mythology</note>, and such things. They are also accustomed, when they can no longer prevail against the **Priest**, to call upon their accomplices for help, either because they are emboldened by their presence—just as a man is by the presence of a friend—or because they are aided in some other hidden way.

<margin>Diligence to be observed against demons.</margin>
Against this, however, the **Priest** should take care to learn which ones are his greatest enemies in heaven or in hell, and indeed to invoke the Saints frequently, while conjuring the enemy demons against him, and forbidding his friends by command from coming to his aid. They are also

<margin>Demons willingly seek to hide.</margin>
wont to willingly withdraw themselves from the mouth of the possessed person, through which they were speaking, and hide in the heart, or other parts below the neck; but the **Priest**, as much as he can, should take care to keep the spirit in the upper parts <note>i.e., the throat and mouth, to ensure the demon can still be forced to speak or depart</note>; for which purpose these words are most effective:

> "Behold the Cross of the Lord, flee you adverse powers!" <note>original: *Ecce Crucem Domini, fugite partes adversæ*</note>

along with the sign of the cross upon the forehead or head of the possessed; and that Psalm,

> "How long, O Lord, will you forget me forever?" <note>Psalm 13; original: *Usquequo Domine oblivisceris me in finem*</note>

and likewise a command made that he should respond concerning something; whereby, even if the **Exorcism** has been interrupted, he must be called back to the upper parts. Different

<margin>Different demons are afflicted by different sacred words.</margin>
demons, however, are more greatly afflicted by different sacred words; and are made more ready to obey. They are also accustomed, when about to depart, for some of them to remain behind so that, as if holding possession, they may again reintroduce those who were expelled; but let the **Priest** be cautious, commanding them to take their whole damned company with them. After they have departed (according to what the Savior says), they seek to enter again and wonderfully tempt the liberated person, even in small matters, especially concerning modesty <note>original: *honestatem*; in this context, it refers to moral purity or chastity</note>. Because sometimes

<margin>Demons re-enter human bodies because of venial sins.</margin>
they re-enter even because of venial sins <note>In Catholic theology, venial sins are lesser sins that do not fully separate a person from God's grace, as opposed to mortal sins.</note>. And therefore, let the liberated person watch in silence and prayers for many months: and let them communicate <note>Receive the sacrament of the Eucharist/Holy Communion</note> very frequently, at least on every feast day; and if the person is married, let them lead their partner to continence <note>Abstinence from sexual relations</note> as much as possible for some time; but if they cannot, at least
